On the wave turbulence theory of 2D gravity waves, I: deterministic energy estimates Thanks: Y. D. was supported in part by NSF DMS-1900251 and a Sloan Fellowship. A. D. I. was supported in part by NSF grant DMS-2007008 and a Simons Collaboration Grant on Weak Turbulence. F. P. was supported in part by a start-up grant from the University of Toronto, and NSERC grant RGPIN-2018-06487.
Yu Deng, Alexandru D. Ionescu, Fabio Pusateri

Abstract
. Our goal in this paper is to initiate the rigorous investigation of wave turbulence and derivation of wave kinetic equations (WKE) for water waves models. This problem has received intense attention in recent years in the context of semilinear models, such as Schrödinger equations [ 10 , 12 , 16 , 17 , 22 , 23 , 24 ] or multi-dimensional KdV-type equations [ 47 , 43 ] . However, our situation here is different since the water waves equations are quasilinear and solutions cannot be constructed by iteration of the Duhamel formula due to unavoidable derivative loss. This is the first of two papers in which we design a new strategy to address this issue. We investigate solutions of the gravity water waves system in two dimensions. In the irrotational case this system can be reduced to an evolution equation on the one-dimensional interface, which is a large torus 𝕋 R {\mathbb{T}}_{R} of size R ≥ 1 R\geq 1 . Our first main result is a deterministic energy inequality which provides control of (possibly large) Sobolev norms of solutions for long times, under the condition that a certain L ∞ L^{\infty} -type norm is small. This energy inequality is of “quintic” type: if the L ∞ L^{\infty} norm is O ⁡ ( ε ) O(\varepsilon) , then the increment of the high order energies is controlled for times of the order ε − 3 \varepsilon^{-3} . This is consistent with the approximate quartic integrability of the system, as proved recently in [ 9 , 55 ] . In the second paper in this sequence we will show how to use this energy estimate and a propagation of randomness argument to prove a probabilistic regularity result up to times of the order ε − 3 ​ R 3 / 2 − {\varepsilon}^{-3}R^{3/2-} , in a suitable scaling regime relating ε → 0 {\varepsilon}\rightarrow 0 and R → ∞ R\rightarrow\infty . For our second main result we combine the quintic energy inequality with a bootstrap argument using a suitable Z Z -norm of Strichartz-type to prove that deterministic solutions with Sobolev data of size ε ≪ 1 {\varepsilon}\ll 1 are regular for times of the order ε − 3 ​ min ⁡ ( ε − 3 , R 3 / 4 ) {\varepsilon}^{-3}\min\big({\varepsilon}^{-3},R^{3/4}\big) . In particular, on the real line solutions exist for times of order O ⁡ ( ε − 6 ) O(\varepsilon^{-6}) . This improves substantially on the earlier extended lifespan results with times of order O ⁡ ( ε − 2 ) O(\varepsilon^{-2}) (proved in [ 53 , 35 , 5 , 32 ] ) or of order O ⁡ ( ε − 3 ) O(\varepsilon^{-3}) (proved recently in [ 55 ] ) or of order O ⁡ ( ε − 4 ) O(\varepsilon^{-4}) (proved recently in [ 58 ] ).



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Free boundary Euler equations and water waves

The evolution of an inviscid perfect fluid that occupies a domain
\Omega_{t}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, for
n\geq 2
,
at time
t
,
is described by the free boundary incompressible Euler equations.
If
v
and
p
denote the velocity and the pressure of the fluid (with constant density equal to
1
)
at time
t
and position
x\in\Omega_{t}
, these equations are

(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla)v=-\nabla p-ge_{n},\qquad\nabla\cdot v=0,\qquad x\in\Omega_{t},

(1.1)

where
g
is the gravitational constant. The first equation in (
1.1
) is the conservation of momentum equation, while the second is the incompressibility condition.
The free surface
S_{t}:=\partial\Omega_{t}
moves with the normal component of the velocity according to the kinematic boundary condition

\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla\,\,\mbox{is tangent to}\,\,{\bigcup}_{t}S_{t}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{x,t}.

(1.2)

The pressure on the interface is given by

p(x,t)=\sigma\kappa(x,t),\qquad x\in S_{t},

(1.3)

where
\kappa
is the mean-curvature of
S_{t}
and
\sigma\geq 0
is the surface tension coefficient.
In this paper we consider the
gravity water waves system
,
which corresponds to the case when the surface tension coefficient
\sigma

is equal to
0
and, without loss of generality, the gravity coefficient
g
is equal to
1
.

The free boundary Euler equations (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) can be analyzed in various
types of domains
\Omega_{t}
(bounded, periodic, unbounded), for fluids with different
characteristics (with or without vorticity, with gravity and/or surface tension),
or even more complicated scenarios where the moving interface separates two fluids.
In this paper we consider fluid domains described
as regions in the plane below the graph of function over
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}^{d}:=[\mathbb{R}/(2\pi R{\mathbb{Z}})]^{d}
,
R\geq 1
.
We restrict our attention to irrotational flows, i.e.
\rm{{\,\mbox{curl}\,}}v=0
,
in which case one can reduce (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) to a system on the boundary.
More precisely let
h=h(x,t):{\mathbb{T}}_{R}^{d}\times I\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, and let

\displaystyle\Omega_{t}=\{(x,y)\in{\mathbb{T}}_{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\,:y\leq h(x,t)\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad S_{t}=\{(x,y):y=h(x,t)\}.

Let
\Phi
denote the velocity potential,
\nabla_{x,y}\Phi(x,y,t)=v(x,y,t)
for
(x,y)\in\Omega_{t}
.
If
\phi(x,t):=\Phi(x,h(x,t),t)
is the restriction of
\Phi
to the boundary
S_{t}
,
the equations of motion reduce to a system of equations for the functions

h,\phi:{\mathbb{T}}_{R}^{d}\times I\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
:

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}h=G(h)\phi,\\
&\partial_{t}\phi=-h-\dfrac{1}{2}|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}+\dfrac{{\left(G(h)\phi+\nabla_{x}h\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi\right)}^{2}}{2(1+|\nabla_{x}h|^{2})}.\end{cases}

(1.4)

Here

G(h):=\sqrt{1+|\nabla_{x}h|^{2}}\mathcal{N}(h),

(1.5)

and
\mathcal{N}(h)
is the Dirichlet-Neumann map associated to the domain
\Omega_{t}
.

The system (
1.4
) admits the conserved Hamiltonian

\mathcal{H}(h,\phi):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}_{R}^{d}}G(h)\phi\cdot\phi\,dx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}_{R}^{d}}h^{2}\,dx,

(1.6)

which is the sum of the kinetic energy corresponding to the
L^{2}
norm of the velocity
field and the potential energy due to gravity. We refer to
[
48
, chap. 11]

or the book of Lannes
[
42
]
for the derivation of the system (
1.4
).
It was first observed by Zakharov
[
56
]
that (
1.4
) is the Hamiltonian flow associated to (
1.6
).

The local well-posedness theory of water waves systems is well-understood in a variety of different scenarios, due to the contributions of many authors.
Without being exhaustive we mention
[
51
,
52
,
15
,
41
,
13
,
46
,
2
]
and refer the reader to the survey
[
38
, Section
2
]
or to the book of Lannes
[
42
, Chapter 4]

for extensive discussions concerning local well-posedness results for general water waves systems, and a more complete list of references.
In short, for sufficiently regular Sobolev initial data classical smooth solutions exist
on a (small) time interval that depends on the size of the initial data
and the arc-chord constant of the initial interface.

1.2.
Motivation and main ideas

Our work is motivated by the outstanding problem of understanding
rigorously the wave turbulence theory of water waves.
This is a classical problem in Mathematical Physics, going back to work of Peierls

[
45
]
and Hasselmann
[
30
,
31
]
;
we refer the reader to the recent book
[
44
]

for a more complete description of the problem and many examples.

The wave turbulence problem has received intense attention in recent years, mainly in the context of dispersive models such as semilinear Schrödinger equations;
see for example
[
10
,
12
,
16
,
17
,
40
,
22
,
23
,
24
,
47
]
.
The focus in these papers has been the rigorous derivation of the
wave kinetic equation
(WKE).
At the analytical level, this amounts
to understanding precisely the dynamics of solutions corresponding to randomized initial data,
in the large box and weakly nonlinear limit, and on long time intervals.

Our goal here is to initiate a similar investigation for irrotational water waves models.
There is a fundamental difference between water waves, which are quasilinear evolutions,
and semilinear models: the solution cannot be constructed using the Duhamel formula,
due to unavoidable loss of derivatives. In particular, the solution cannot be described using
iterations of the Duhamel formula (Feynman trees), which is the central idea of the analysis of semilinear models.

To address this fundamental difficulty we propose here a new mechanism, based on a combination of two main ingredients:

(1)
Energy estimates:
We prove first estimates on the energy increment
of the highest order derivatives of the solution.
These are
deterministic
estimates, in the sense that they hold for all solutions.
The key point is to show that the increment is controlled by an
L^{\infty}
-based
norm of the solution (not an
L^{2}
-based norm), which can then be exploited effectively
in the analysis of solutions with random initial data.

(2)
Propagation of randomness and pointwise bounds:

The second step consists in proving propagation of randomness
(in a suitable form), and using Khintchine-type arguments to
derive optimal pointwise bounds on randomized solutions in a large box.
For this one can use the iterated Duhamel formula; the point is that losses
of derivatives are allowed at this stage, thanks to the accompanying estimate on the high order energy.

In this paper we complete the first part of this program, for the problem of pure
gravity water waves in two dimensions (1D interface). More precisely we prove a refined energy inequality for solutions of the system (
1.4
) in a large box
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
,
R\geq 1
; see Theorem
1.1
.
The a priori energy inequality (
1.7
)-(
1.8
)
is of “acceptable quintic” type, consistent with the “almost integrability” of the system (
1.4
)
(on
{\mathbb{T}}
) up to order 4; see the works of Dyachenko-Zakharov
[
56
]
,
Craig-Worfolk
[
14
]
and Craig-Sulem
[
13
]
on the “almost integrability” of the system,
and the rigorous proofs by Berti-Feola-Pusateri
[
9
]
and Wu
[
55
]
.
The second part of the program will be completed in our second paper
[
25
]
.

As a byproduct of our analysis, in this paper we also show that we can use this “acceptable quintic energy inequality”
to prove a deterministic improved lifespan
result on solutions of Sobolev size
\varepsilon\ll 1
.
In particular we show that in the Euclidean case
R=\infty
solutions of
the gravity water waves system of Sobolev size
O(\varepsilon)

remain regular on times of order
O(\varepsilon^{-6})
.
This improves significantly on all the earlier enhanced lifespan results,
with times of order
O(\varepsilon^{-2})
(proved in
[
53
,
35
,
5
,
32
]
)
or of order
O(\varepsilon^{-3})
(proved in
[
55
]
) or of order
O(\varepsilon^{-4})
(proved in
[
58
]
).

1.3.
An acceptable quintic energy inequality

Our first main theorem provides a quintic a priori estimate on the increment of energy of solutions
of the gravity water waves system in 2D.

Theorem 1.1
.

Assume that
N_{0}\geq 6
,
N_{1}\in[4,N_{0}-2]
,
T_{1}\leq T_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
(h,\phi)\in C([T_{1},T_{2}]:H^{N_{0}}\times\dot{H}^{N_{0},1/2})

is a solution of the system (
1.4
). Moreover, assume that
1
1

1

See
(
2.25
) for the definition of the spaces
\dot{W}^{N,b}
and
Lemma
3.1
for the definition of the good unknown
\omega=\phi-T_{B}h
.

\begin{split}\|h(t)\|_{H^{N_{0}}}+\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega(t)\|_{H^{N_{0}}}+\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi(t)\|_{H^{N_{0}-1/2}}&\leq A,\\
\|h(t)\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{1},0}}+\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega(t)\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{1},0}}+\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi(t)\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{1}-1/2,0}}&\leq\varepsilon(t),\end{split}

(1.7)

for any
t\in[T_{1},T_{2}]
, where
A\in(0,\infty)
and
\varepsilon:[T_{1},T_{2}]\to[0,\infty)

is a continuous function satisfying
\varepsilon(t)\ll 1
.
Then, for any
t_{1}\leq t_{2}\in[T_{1},T_{2}]
,

\big\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega)(t_{2})\big\|_{H^{N_{0}}}^{2}\lesssim\big\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega)(t_{1})\big\|_{H^{N_{0}}}^{2}+A^{2}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}[\varepsilon(s)]^{3}\,ds,

(1.8)

1.3.1.
Remarks

We make some remarks on the statement of Theorem
1.1
and discuss some of the main ideas of its proof.

(1)
The acceptable quintic increment
.
The main point of the theorem is to show that the energy increment is quintic and acceptable,
that is, the integral term in (
1.8
) involves
A^{2}
and the third power

[\varepsilon(s)]^{3}
of the
L^{\infty}
-based norm.
For future applications when
R
is large, in particular in the random data setting and to the small-data result in Theorem
1.2
below,
we emphasize that it is crucial to distinguish these two quantities and allow
A
(the high Sobolev energy) to be large,
while only
{\varepsilon}(t)
(the controlling
L^{\infty}
norm) is kept small. Acceptable energy increments can contain only 2 copies of the large Sobolev norm of the solution.

Several “quartic” energy inequalities, which improve on the standard cubic
estimates used to establish local well-posedness,
have been proved in the last ten years or so, in both one and two dimensions;
see for example
[
53
,
54
,
35
,
5
,
32
,
57
]
for gravity waves.
The key common ingredient in all of these results is the absence of quadratic resonances.

Berti-Feola-Pusateri
[
8
,
9
]
proved a quintic energy inequality in dimension
d=1
for
R=1
,
as a consequence of the conjugation of the flow of (
1.6
) to its integrable Birkhoff normal form
at order
4
. More recently, Wu
[
55
]
also proved a quintic energy inequality for the problem on
\mathbb{R}
.

Our quintic energy inequality (
1.8
) in the case
R=\infty
is not directly comparable
with the result of Wu. Our main concern, motivated by applications to random data
problems and wave turbulence, is to show that the energy
increment can be bounded using 3 copies of the
L^{\infty}
-type norm in (
1.7
),
even for solutions of large (high-order) energy; this is what we call
acceptable
.
The focus of
[
55
]
instead is to prove a quintic energy inequality
for energy-small initial data at improved levels of regularity and allow for the presence of steep waves.

(2)
Relevance to the random data problem in the large box limit
.
As explained earlier, our main goal is to apply Theorem
1.1
to investigate the
energy distribution for large times of
solutions of (
1.4
) with randomized initial data, in the limit
R\rightarrow\infty
.
To do this, we first need to construct smooth solutions on long time intervals. While long-time
or even global regularity is not an issue in many semilinear problems, even at the deterministic level,
this becomes a significant difficulty in quasilinear problems, where conservation laws are
scaling-supercritical and cannot be used.
High-order energy inequalities such as (
1.8
) are essentially the only known tool available
in the quasilinear setting to propagate regularity.

Roughly speaking, the randomization of the data can give enhanced control on
L^{\infty}
-type norms. More precisely, for a solution with Sobolev norm of size
A
one can hope to prove that, with high probability, the
L^{\infty}
-norm is of size
\varepsilon\approx AR^{-1/2}
, consistent with the conservation of mass
and the solution being uniformly distributed over
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
.
In principle, one can hope to prove such an enhanced
L^{\infty}
estimate by propagating randomness (using Feynman’s diagram expansions in the spirit of the recent works on NLS cited earlier), and combine this with the deterministic energy estimate (
1.8
) to construct regular solutions up to times of the order
{\varepsilon}^{-3}
.
We will prove a rigorous result of this type in the second paper of this sequence
[
25
]
.

(3)
Quadratic resonances.

The main obstruction to obtaining an energy inequality of quintic type is the presence
of quadratic and cubic resonances in the system.
It is well-known that (in any dimension) there are no non-trivial quadratic resonances
(or resonant three-waves interactions) for (
1.4
);
that is, there are no numbers
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
solving

\sigma_{1}|\xi_{1}|^{1/2}+\sigma_{2}|\xi_{2}|^{1/2}+\sigma_{3}|\xi_{3}|^{1/2}=0,\qquad\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}=0,

(1.9)

where
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\in\{-1,1\}
.
This algebraic fact implies that the quadratic nonlinearities are inessential to the dynamics
and can be (at least formally) eliminated by normal forms or time-averaging.
Using appropriate variables, such as the “good unknown”
\omega
, or
Riemann mapping or holomorphic coordinates,
one can avoid losses of derivatives in normal form procedures
and obtain an “acceptable quartic” energy inequality of the form

\big\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega)(t_{2})\big\|_{H^{N_{0}}}^{2}\lesssim\big\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega)(t_{1})\big\|_{H^{N_{0}}}^{2}+A^{2}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}[\varepsilon(s)]^{2}\,ds.

(1.10)

Basic inequalities of this type have been proven in various ways in several works,
including the already cited
[
53
,
54
,
35
,
37
,
4
,
33
,
34
,
57
]
;
for a longer discussion and more references also about the Klein-Gordon equation
and parameter dependent PDEs, see §1.4 in
[
39
]
.

(4)
Cubic resonances.
To improve (
1.10
) and obtain (
1.8
)
one needs to look at “cubic resonances” (or resonant 4-waves interactions)
that is, solutions of the system

\sigma_{1}|\xi_{1}|^{1/2}+\sigma_{2}|\xi_{2}|^{1/2}+\sigma_{3}|\xi_{3}|^{1/2}+\sigma_{4}|\xi_{4}|^{1/2}=0,\qquad\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}+\xi_{4}=0,

(1.11)

for
\xi_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
and
\sigma_{j}\in\{-1,1\}
,
j=1,\dots 4
.
Equation (
1.11
) has a set of trivial solutions of the form
(\xi,\eta,-\xi,-\eta)
with
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}=1
,

\sigma_{3}=\sigma_{4}=-1
,
together with their natural permutations; these are the so-called “trivial resonances”
and cannot be avoided.
However, due to the Hamiltonian/reversible nature of the equations, these resonances give
an integrable contribution and do not appear in the energy increment.
We refer the reader to the introduction of
[
9
]
for a more detailed discussion about this.

Moreover, a simple argument shows that (
1.11
) has exactly one other two-parameter family of solutions (up to natural symmetries),
the so called
Benjamin-Feir resonances

[
28
,
14
,
27
]
, which are given by
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4})=(1,1,-1,-1)
and

\mathcal{R}_{BF}:=\bigcup_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\},\,b\in[0,\infty)}\Big\{\xi_{1}=-\lambda(b^{2}+b+1)^{2},\,\xi_{2}=\lambda b^{2},\,\xi_{3}=\lambda(b+1)^{2},\,\xi_{4}=\lambda(b+1)^{2}b^{2}\Big\}.

(1.12)

The resonances (
1.12
) are a potential obstruction to proving quintic energy inequalities.
The key point is that one can show that the contribution of these resonances to the energy increment also vanishes.
This is due to the fact that the symbol of the cubic interactions (after the first normal form step)
vanishes on the set (
1.12
), after suitable symmetrizations.
This algebraic fact was already known since the works
[
28
,
14
,
27
,
13
]
.

However, substantial work is needed to turn this into a rigorous quintic energy inequality.
The first step is to calculate precisely the quartic Fourier multipliers appearing in our problem
after performing the first normal form; see Lemmas
4.1
,
4.6
, and
4.7
.
In order to perform a second normal form step and obtain acceptable quintic expressions,
we need to identify a proper class of “admissible symbols” (see Definition
5.1
),
which allows certain types of singularities. These singularities are present in our problem and cannot be avoided;
such singularities could in principle lead to quintic bulk terms
containing Hilbert transforms acting on the product of two unknowns,
and preclude the basic inequality (
1.8
).
The point is that this does not happen in our case due to the special structure of the equation.

Finally we need to verify that the symbols vanish at Benjamin-Feir resonant sites defined in (
1.12
), and justify the division.
For this we rely instead on an explicit algebraic computation, which is done in Section
9
and requires full symmetrization of all the symbols. The symbols of the quartic bulks obtained in this way are long algebraic expressions with no clear simplifications, and the calculations necessary to verify the vanishing
at the non-trivial resonances are lengthy, but manageable.
2
2

2

This vanishing is expected, however, for conceptual reasons, as it is already known to hold at the formal level (the key observation of Zakharov and Dyatchenko
[
56
]
), which corresponds to setting
\chi=0
. We need a nontrivial function
\chi
in our paper, in order to define correctly paradifferential operators, which increases substantially the complexity of the calculation, but this should be thought of as a technical tool needed to avoid derivative loss which does not affect the intrinsic structure of the system.
Once vanishing is proved, the division can be justified easily due to the smoothness properties of the symbols.

(5)
Eulerian coordinates.
In this paper we work in Eulerian coordinates. While other choices are possible
(such as Lagrangian coordinates as in
[
53
,
55
]
or holomorphic coordinates as in
[
32
,
34
]
),
at the conceptual level Eulerian coordinates are the natural and robust choice to analyze small-amplitude waves,
as they apply equally well to both two and three-dimensional problems.
This choice leads to slightly longer calculations compared to other choices,
but these calculations are explicit and can be performed without any computer assistance.

(6)
Classes of symbols.
At a basic level, many of our energy estimates rely on controlling quartic expressions of the form

I_{M}:=\Big|\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{3}}\sum_{(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4})\in\mathbb{H}^{3}_{R}}\widehat{h_{1}}(\xi_{1})\widehat{h_{2}}(\xi_{2})\widehat{h_{3}}(\xi_{3})\widehat{h_{4}}(\xi_{4})M(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4})\Big|,

(1.13)

where
h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4}
are functions related to water waves variables and
\mathbb{H}^{3}_{R}:=\{(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4})\in({\mathbb{Z}}/R)^{4}:\,\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}+\xi_{4}=0\}
. Such expressions appear many times when estimating bulk contributions (in the Fourier space), for various multipliers
M
. In our applications, we have control of the functions
h_{l}
in
L^{2}
-based Sobolev norms,
\|h_{l}\|_{H^{N_{0}}}\leq A_{2}
, as well as in
L^{\infty}
-based Sobolev norms,
\|h_{l}\|_{\widetilde{W}^{2}}\leq A_{\infty}
, and we would like to prove sharp bounds of the form

I_{M}\lesssim A_{2}^{2}A_{\infty}^{2}.

(1.14)

In order to prove such bounds (uniformly in
R
) we need good information on the symbols
M
. The ideal situation is when the symbols
M
are
regular
, as described precisely in Definition
4.4
, satisfying suitable differential bounds of the Hörmander-Michlin type. But this is insufficient
3
3

3

These considerations are important only as
R\to\infty
, in order to prove bounds that are uniform in
R
. When
R
is bounded, differentiation of symbols is not needed and one can use just pointwise bounds to measure symbols. At a more basic level,
L^{\infty}
-based norms in the physical space are not needed at all when
R
is bounded, since
L^{\infty}
is in fact stronger than
L^{2}
in this case. This effectively eliminates one of the key difficulties of the problem, namely the requirement that acceptable energy estimates can contain only two copies of the (large) Sobolev norms.
in our case: while many of our symbols are indeed regular, the application of normal forms leads to symbols that contain factors of the form
|\xi_{i}+\xi_{j}|
, which do not obey suitable derivative bounds. To deal with these contributions we need to define a special class of symbols, which we call
admissible
, see Definition
5.1
, which are well adapted to our problem. These symbols are more general than the regular symbols, but still lead to optimal quartic bounds like (
1.14
) and retain sufficient smoothness (after factoring out the singular factors) to allow for symmetrization and control of small divisors.

(7)
Small divisors.
One of the main issues in implementing normal form procedures is the loss of derivatives
that could occur when dividing by resonant phases.
On a fixed torus
[
9
]
these losses can be avoided thanks to
paradifferential reductions to smoothing operators.
In this paper we do not reduce matters to smoothing operators
(and this may not be actually possible uniformly as
R\rightarrow\infty
).
Our strategy here is different, based on using explicit formulas and identifying suitable properties of the symbols. The general idea is that if one knows that a symbol (1) vanishes at a resonant site
and (2) satisfies suitable differential bounds (such as those satisfied by regular symbols and by admissible symbols),
then one can solve the division problem with no loss of derivatives.
For a relatively simple implementation of this idea
we refer the reader to Step 2 in the proof of Lemma
5.3
,
which deals with the frequency configuration where Benjamin-Feir resonances are present
and the symbols are regular.
The implementation in the case of the trivial resonances in the subsequent steps is more involved and uses
the full structure of the admissible class of symbols.

(8)
Symmetrization of symbols.
Effective estimates, for example on quartic expressions like (
1.13
), can only be proved after accounting and exploiting the symmetries of the problem. For example, if
h_{1}=h_{2}
then one can replace the symbol
M
with its symmetrized version
[M(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4})+M(\xi_{2},\xi_{1},\xi_{3},\xi_{4})]/2
in the first two variables.

In our proof, symmetrization serves three basic purposes: (i) in certain cases it eliminates the derivative loss coming from the quasi-linear nature of the problem and normal forms, (ii) it allows us to verify the key vanishing condition of the quartic symbols at the Benjamin-Feir resonant sites, and (iii) at a more subtle level, it prevents the emergence of worse singularities at low frequencies (like
\mathrm{sgn}(\xi_{i}+\xi_{j})
which appear naturally before symmetrization due to normal forms and Hilbert transforms) which would ultimately prevent us from proving (
1.8
).

(9)
Optimality.

Finally, we note that a quintic energy inequality like (
1.8
)
is expected to be optimal in terms of homogeneity in
{\varepsilon}(t)
,
in view of the presence of quintic resonances as exhibited by Craig-Worfolk
[
14
]

and Dyachenko-Lvov-Zakharov
[
28
]
.

1.4.
Small data long-term regularity

As discussed above, Theorem
1.1
is the first of two main steps
towards understanding the energy distribution for gravity water waves with random initial data.
In addition, we can also utilize it in the case of deterministic
initial data with small Sobolev norm, to prove new extended lifespan results.
More precisely we have:

Theorem 1.2
.

Assume that
N_{0}\geq 7
and
(h_{0},\phi_{0})\in H^{N_{0}+1/2}({\mathbb{T}}_{R})\times H^{N_{0}+1/2}({\mathbb{T}}_{R})

is initial data satisfying the assumptions

{\|\,\langle\partial_{x}\rangle^{1/2}h_{0}\|}_{H^{N_{0}}}+{\|\,|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi_{0}\|}_{H^{N_{0}}}\leq{\varepsilon}\leq 1.

(1.15)

Then there is a unique solution
(h,\phi)\in C([0,T_{R,\varepsilon}]:H^{N_{0}}\times\dot{H}^{N_{0},1/2})

of the system (
1.4
) on
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}\times[0,T_{R,\varepsilon}]
, with initial data
(h(0),\phi(0))=(h_{0},\phi_{0})
,
where

T_{R,\varepsilon}\gtrsim{\varepsilon}^{-3}\min\big({\varepsilon}^{-3},R^{3/4}\big).

(1.16)

Moreover, letting
\omega=\phi-T_{B}h
as in (
3.3
), the solution satisfies the bounds

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T_{R,\varepsilon}]}

\displaystyle{\big\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega)(t)\big\|}_{H^{N_{0}}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}.

(1.17)

1.4.1.
Remarks and main ideas

The main point of Theorem
1.2
is the enhanced lifespan of solutions corresponding
to small initial data in Sobolev spaces, without any additional localization. This problem has been considered by many authors and for many dispersive models. We discuss below some related results in this direction, and some of the main ideas of our proof.

(1)
The case of a fixed-size torus
.
When
R\approx 1
, the local well-posedness theory
gives a time of existence of
O(\varepsilon^{-1})
.
Existence times of the order
O(\varepsilon^{-2})
for gravity waves
follow the from the quartic energy inequalities in the already cited
[
53
,
35
,
5
,
32
]
.
Similar results have also been proved for capillary waves (
g=0,\sigma>0
) in
[
37
,
34
]
and gravity-capillary waves (
g,\sigma>0
) in
[
7
]
.

The more recent papers
[
9
,
55
]
provided the first regularity results up to times
O({\varepsilon}^{-3})
.
Theorem
1.2
matches these results in the case of a fixed torus
R\approx 1
, but improves significantly for large tori
R\gg 1
.
This result is probably optimal for general Sobolev data due to the presence of resonant

5
-wave interactions, see
[
14
,
27
]
.

Extended lifespan results have also been proved for many other dispersive and hyperbolic models.
Without attempting to be exhaustive, we mention the monograph of Berti-Delort
[
6
]
,
who obtained regularity up to times
O({\varepsilon}^{-N})
, for any fixed
N
, for standing gravity-capillary waves over
{\mathbb{T}}
,
for almost all values
of the surface tension and gravity parameters.
In general, longer existence times can be achieved by iterating normal forms procedures,
when non-trivial resonances can be avoided for almost all values of external parameters.
See also the work of Delort on Klein-Gordon equations
[
20
]
and references therein.
In the case
d=2
, an extended
O(\varepsilon^{-2})
lifespan for pure gravity waves
is obtained implicitly in the work of Wu
[
54
]
, and is included
in the more general results of Zheng
[
57
]
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{R}
.
For the gravity-capillary case on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
an extended lifespan
for almost all values of the parameters
(g,\sigma)
was obtained in
[
39
]
.

(2)
Large
R
and the Euclidean case
. If
R\gtrsim{\varepsilon}^{-4}
or in the Euclidean case
R=\infty
,
we obtain a time of existence
T_{R,\varepsilon}\approx\varepsilon^{-6}
.
This improves substantially on all the previous results on 2D water waves with Sobolev initial data,
including the recent
O({\varepsilon}^{-3})
lifespan result of
[
55
]
for
R=\infty
based on a quartic-type energy inequality,
and the very recent
O({\varepsilon}^{-2}\min({\varepsilon}^{-2},\sqrt{R}))

result of Zheng
[
58
]
which uses the simpler quartic energy inequality (
1.10
) and a version of a Strichartz norm of the form (
1.18
). Similar extended lifespan results,
depending on the size
\varepsilon
of the initial data and the size
R
of the periodic box,
have been proved recently by Zheng
[
57
]
in the case of pure gravity waves in 3D.

We mention that in the Euclidean case
R=\infty
,
for localized initial data in a weighted Sobolev space, one can actually prove full global
regularity and scattering results,
relying on dispersion and pointwise decay.
We refer the reader to
[
53
,
35
,
5
,
33
,
49
]
for the case of
1
d interfaces,
and to
[
54
,
29
,
26
,
50
]
for
2
d interfaces (see
[
38
, Section 3]

for an overview of these results). However, localization is not compatible with randomized initial data,
so these results are not applicable to the problem of understanding wave turbulence.

(3)
Ideas of the proof
.
Our proof of Theorem
1.2
is based on what we call
the
Z
-norm method
.
This general method is essentially a bootstrap argument
that combines high-order energy estimates with dispersive and decay
estimates at a lower order of regularity.
In our problem the key
Z=Z_{\varepsilon,R}
-norm is defined by means of the following Strichartz-type norm

\displaystyle{\|F\|}_{Z}:=\sup_{t_{1}\leq t_{2}\in[0,T],\,|t_{2}-t_{1}|\leq\,\min({\varepsilon}^{-4},R)}

\displaystyle{\big\|F\big\|}_{L^{4}_{[t_{1},t_{2}]}\dot{W}_{x}^{N_{1}-1/4,0}},

(1.18)

for any function
F\in C([0,T]:H^{N_{0}}({\mathbb{T}}_{R}))
, where
N_{1}=N_{0}-3\geq 4
.
See the second norm in (
7.11
), and (
2.25
), for the exact definition of the norm.

The main new ingredient is the Strichartz estimates in Lemma
7.2
,
which provide sharp
L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}
decay of linear solutions in Sobolev spaces.
We can then combine these Strichartz estimates (which are, essentially,
efficient substitutes for Sobolev’s embedding) and the control on high Sobolev norms from (
1.8
),
to propagate regularity of solutions on the time interval
[0,T_{R,\varepsilon}]
,
with
T_{R,\varepsilon}
as in (
1.16
).

1.5.
Organization

In Section
2
we collect several basic definitions and results,
including some classes of multipliers that we will encounter, multilinear product estimates,
the definitions of our function spaces, and elements of pseudo-differential calculus.

In Section
3
we paralinearize of the water waves system, using
the good unknown of Alinhac and following the general approach of Alazard-Burq-Zuily
[
2
,
3
]

and our paralinearization of the Dirichlet-Neumann (DN) operator in Appendix B of
[
37
]
.
The proofs of the main technical results, Lemmas
3.1
and
3.2
,
are postponed to Section
8
.
The equations that are eventually derived in Lemma
3.4

for the good variables
(h^{\ast},\omega^{\ast})
are the starting point for our analysis.

In Section
4
we define our energy functional (
4.4
)
and provide a bound for its increment in Lemma
4.1
, in terms of quartic “bulk terms”;
this corresponds to a first normal form. We then rewrite these quartic bulk integrals in terms of our main variables
(h^{\ast},\omega^{\ast})
.
These are mostly straightforward algebraic calculations,
but need to be performed very accurately in order to be able to verify the
necessary vanishing conditions at the non-trivial resonances later on.

In Section
5
we prove acceptable quintic bounds for quartic bulk integrals,
under suitable assumptions on the defining symbols. The key issue is to identify a suitable class
of “admissible multipliers” (see Definition
5.1
),
sufficiently large to capture the multipliers encountered in our problem,
but restrictive enough to allow an additional normal form. The main result of this section, Lemma
5.3
,
gives the desired quintic-type bounds in the resonant case,
provided that certain vanishing and regularity conditions are met.

The main acceptable quintic energy inequality (
1.8
) is obtained in Subsection
6.2
,
as a consequence of the work done in Sections
9
and
6

to verify the necessary admissibility and vanishing conditions for the symbols close to both sets of resonances.
To deal with the Benjamin-Feir resonances we need to pass to complex variables and fully symmetrize the expressions.

In Section
7
we prove Theorem
1.2
.
We first prove Strichartz estimates for the linear flow
e^{it|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}}
on
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}

in Lemma
7.2

and then bootstrap the
Z
-norm (
1.18
) in Subsection
7.2
.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we summarize some of our main notation and prove a few lemmas that are used later in the paper.

2.1.
The Fourier transform and Littlewood-Paley projections

For functions
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{R})
we define the Fourier transform

\widehat{f}(\xi)=\mathcal{F}(f)(\xi):=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{R}}f(x)e^{-i\xi x}\,dx,\qquad\xi\in\mathbb{Z}/R:=\{n/R:\,n\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

(2.1)

The definition shows easily that

\begin{split}f(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}/R}\widehat{f}(\xi)e^{i\xi x},\\
\int_{\mathbb{T}_{R}}f(x)\overline{g(x)}\,dx=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}/R}\widehat{f}(\xi)\overline{\widehat{g}(\xi)},\\
\mathcal{F}(fg)(\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta\in\mathbb{Z}/R}\widehat{f}(\xi-\eta)\widehat{g}(\eta).\end{split}

(2.2)

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
let
k^{+}:=\max(k,0)
and
k^{-}:=\min(k,0)
. We fix an even smooth function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
supported in
[-8/5,8/5]
and equal to
1
in
[-5/4,5/4]
,
and define

\varphi_{k}(x):=\varphi(x/2^{k})-\varphi(x/2^{k-1}),\qquad\varphi_{\leq k}(x):=\varphi(x/2^{k}),\qquad\varphi_{\geq k}(x):=1-\varphi(x/2^{k-1}),

for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Let
P_{k}
,
P_{\leq k}
, and
P_{\geq k}
denote the operators on
\mathbb{T}_{R}
defined by the Fourier multipliers
\varphi_{k}
,

\varphi_{\leq k}
, and
\varphi_{\geq k}
respectively.
Moreover, let

P^{\prime}_{k}:=\sum_{a\in\{-2,-1,0,1,2\}}P_{k+a}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\varphi^{\prime}_{k}:=\sum_{a\in\{-2,-1,0,1,2\}}\varphi_{k+a}

(2.3)

Notice that
P_{k}=0
if
2^{k}R\leq 1/2
.
We also let
\overline{{\mathbb{Z}}}:={\mathbb{Z}}\cup\{-\infty\}
and define the operator
P_{-\infty}
by

P_{-\infty}f(x):=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}_{R}}f(y)\,dy.

The operators
P_{k}
are given by convolution in the physical space.
More precisely, for
f\in L^{1}({\mathbb{T}}_{R})
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

\begin{split}P_{k}f(x)&=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}_{R}}f(y)K_{k}(x-y),\\
K_{k}(x)&:=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\xi\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}\varphi_{k}(\xi)e^{ix\xi}=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\varphi_{0}(n/(R2^{k}))e^{i(x/R)n},\end{split}

(2.4)

as a consequence of the Fourier inversion formula in (
2.2
). The kernels
K_{k}
can be estimated using the Poisson summation formula,

K_{k}(x)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{2\pi R}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi_{0}(\eta/(R2^{k}))e^{i(x/R)\eta}e^{-i2\pi n\eta}\,d\eta=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}2^{k}\widetilde{\varphi_{0}}\big(2^{k}(x-2\pi nR)\big),

(2.5)

where
\widetilde{\varphi_{0}}
denotes the Euclidean inverse Fourier transform of
\varphi_{0}
. In particular
\|K_{k}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{T}}_{R})}\lesssim 1
for any
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and therefore the operators
P_{k}
are uniformly bounded on
L^{p}
,
p\in[1,\infty]
.

2.2.
Multipliers and associated operators

We will often work with multipliers
m:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
or
m:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{C}
, and operators defined by such multipliers. We define the class of symbols

S^{\infty}:=\{m:({\mathbb{Z}}/R)^{d}\to\mathbb{C}:\,{\|m\|}_{S^{\infty}}:={\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}(m)\|}_{L^{1}({\mathbb{T}}_{R}^{d})}<\infty\}.

(2.6)

Given a suitable symbol
m
we define the associated bilinear operator
M
by

\mathcal{F}\big[M(f,g)\big](\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta\in\mathbb{Z}/R}m(\xi-\eta,\eta)\widehat{f}(\xi-\eta)\widehat{g}(\eta).

(2.7)

For
d\geq 2
we define also the hyperplanes

\begin{split}&\mathbb{H}^{d}_{R}:=\{(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d+1})\in({\mathbb{Z}}/R)^{d+1}:\,\xi_{1}+\ldots+\xi_{d+1}=0\},\\
&\mathbb{H}^{d}_{\infty}:=\{(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d+1})\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}:\,\xi_{1}+\ldots+\xi_{d+1}=0\},\end{split}

(2.8)

and the associated class of symbols

\begin{split}&\widetilde{S}^{\infty}=\widetilde{S}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{d}_{R}):=\big\{m:\mathbb{H}_{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}:\\
&{\|m\|}_{\widetilde{S}^{\infty}}:=\Big\|\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{d}}\sum_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d}\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}m(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d},-\xi_{1}-\ldots-\xi_{d})e^{i(x_{1}\xi_{1}+\ldots+x_{d}\xi_{d})}\Big\|_{L^{1}_{x}({\mathbb{T}}_{R}^{d})}<\infty\big\}.\end{split}

(2.9)

Lemma
2.1
below summarizes some properties of symbols and associated operators.

Lemma 2.1
.

With the notation (
2.6
) and (
2.9
) we have the following:

(i) Assume
p,q,r\in[1,\infty]
satisfy
1/p+1/q=1/r
, and
m\in S^{\infty}
. Then

\|M(f,g)\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{T}_{R})}\lesssim\|m\|_{S^{\infty}}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}_{R})}\|g\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{T}_{R})}.

(2.10)

for any
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{R})
.

(ii) More generally, if
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\in[1,\infty]
,
n\geq 3
,
are exponents that satisfy
1/p_{1}+\ldots+1/p_{n}=1
and
m\in\widetilde{S}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{n-1}_{R})
then

\begin{split}\Big|\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{n-1}}\sum_{(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n})\in\mathbb{H}^{n-1}_{R}}\widehat{f_{1}}(\xi_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\widehat{f_{n}}(\xi_{n})m(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n})\Big|\\
\lesssim\|f_{1}\|_{L^{p_{1}}(\mathbb{T}_{R})}\cdot\ldots\cdot\|f_{n}\|_{L^{p_{n}}(\mathbb{T}_{R})}\|m\|_{\widetilde{S}^{\infty}}.\end{split}

(2.11)

Proof.

The bounds (
2.10
) follow from (
2.11
) with
n=3
. To prove (
2.11
) we rewrite

\begin{split}\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{n-1}}&\sum_{(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n})\in\mathbb{H}^{n-1}_{R}}\widehat{f_{1}}(\xi_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\widehat{f_{n}}(\xi_{n})m(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n})\\
&=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}_{R}^{n}}f_{1}(x_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot f_{n}(x_{n})K(x_{n}-x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}-x_{n-1})\,dx_{1}\ldots dx_{n}\\
&=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}_{R}^{n}}f_{1}(z-y_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot f_{n-1}(z-y_{n-1})f_{n}(z)K(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})\,dy_{1}\ldots dy_{n-1}dz,\end{split}

where

K(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1}):=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{n-1}}\sum_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n-1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}m(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n-1},-\xi_{1}-\ldots-\xi_{n-1})e^{i(y_{1}\xi_{1}+\ldots+y_{n-1}\xi_{n-1})}.

Since
\|K\|_{L^{1}}={\|m\|}_{\widetilde{S}^{\infty}}
the desired bounds (
2.11
) follow using
Hölder’s inequality in
z
.
∎

To apply Lemma
2.1
we need to bound the
S^{\infty}
and
\widetilde{S}^{\infty}
norms of various multipliers.
We will often use the following simple properties:

Lemma 2.2
.

The following hold true:

(i) If
m,m^{\prime}\in S^{\infty}
then
m\cdot m^{\prime}\in S^{\infty}
and

\|m\cdot m^{\prime}\|_{S^{\infty}}\lesssim\|m\|_{S^{\infty}}\|m^{\prime}\|_{S^{\infty}}.

(2.12)

Similarly, if
m,m^{\prime}\in\widetilde{S}^{\infty}
then
m\cdot m^{\prime}\in\widetilde{S}^{\infty}
and

\|m\cdot m^{\prime}\|_{\widetilde{S}^{\infty}}\lesssim\|m\|_{\widetilde{S}^{\infty}}\|m^{\prime}\|_{\widetilde{S}^{\infty}}.

(2.13)

Moreover, if
d\geq 2
,
\sigma:\{1,\ldots,d+1\}\to\{1,\ldots,d+1\}
is a permutation,
m\in\widetilde{S}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}_{R}^{d})
, and
m_{\sigma}(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d+1}):=m(\xi_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,\xi_{\sigma(d+1)})
then

\|m_{\sigma}\|_{\widetilde{S}^{\infty}}=\|m\|_{\widetilde{S}^{\infty}}.

(2.14)

(ii) Assume that
d\geq 1
and
m:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
is a continuous compactly supported function and
\check{m}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the Euclidean inverse Fourier transform of
m
. Then

\Big\|\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{d}}\sum_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d}\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}m(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d})e^{i(x_{1}\xi_{1}+\ldots+x_{d}\xi_{d})}\Big\|_{L^{1}_{x}({\mathbb{T}}_{R}^{d})}\lesssim\|\check{m}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

(2.15)

(iii) In particular, assume that
\underline{k}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and let
\varphi_{\underline{k}}(\xi):=\varphi_{k_{1}}(\xi_{1})\ldots\varphi_{k_{d}}(\xi_{d})
. Assume that the multiplier
m:({\mathbb{Z}}/R)^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
admits an extension
m^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
(such that
m(\xi)\varphi_{\underline{k}}(\xi)=m^{\prime}(\xi)\varphi_{\underline{k}}(\xi)
for all
\xi\in({\mathbb{Z}}/R)^{d}
) satisfying the differential inequalities

\big|2^{\alpha\cdot\underline{k}}\partial^{\alpha}_{\xi}\big[m^{\prime}(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d})\varphi_{k_{1}}(\xi_{1})\ldots\varphi_{k_{d}}(\xi_{d})\big]\big|\lesssim\Lambda

(2.16)

and for any multi-index
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d})
with
|\alpha|:=\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{d}\leq(d+2)/2
and any
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d}\in\mathbb{R}
, where
2^{\alpha\cdot\underline{k}}=2^{\alpha_{1}k_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{d}k_{d}}
,
\partial^{\alpha}_{\xi}=\partial^{\alpha_{1}}_{\xi_{1}}\ldots\partial^{\alpha_{d}}_{\xi_{d}}
, and
\Lambda\in(0,\infty)
. Then

\big\|m(\xi)\varphi_{\underline{k}}(\xi)\big\|_{S^{\infty}}\lesssim\Lambda.

(2.17)

The conclusion also holds if one replaces some of the functions
\varphi_{k_{j}}(\xi_{j})
with
\varphi_{\leq k_{j}}(\xi_{j})
in both the assumption (
2.16
) and the conclusion (
2.17
).

(iv) Similarly, if
\underline{k}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d+1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d+1}
,
\Lambda\in(0,\infty)
, and the multiplier
m:\mathbb{H}^{d}_{R}\to\mathbb{C}
admits an extension
m^{\prime\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\to\mathbb{C}
(such that
m(\xi)\varphi_{\underline{k}}(\xi)=m^{\prime\prime}(\xi)\varphi_{\underline{k}}(\xi)
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{R}^{d}
) that satisfies the differential inequalities

\big|2^{\alpha\cdot\underline{k}}\partial^{\alpha}_{\xi}\big[m^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d+1})\varphi_{k_{1}}(\xi_{1})\ldots\varphi_{k_{d+1}}(\xi_{d+1})\big]\big|\lesssim\Lambda

(2.18)

and for any multi-index
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d+1})
with
|\alpha|\leq(d+2)/2
and any
\xi\in\mathbb{H}^{d}_{\infty}
, then

\big\|m(\xi)\varphi_{\underline{k}}(\xi)\big\|_{\widetilde{S}^{\infty}}\lesssim\Lambda.

(2.19)

The conclusion also holds if one replaces some of the functions
\varphi_{k_{j}}(\xi_{j})

with
\varphi_{\leq k_{j}}(\xi_{j})
in both the assumption (
2.18
) and the conclusion (
2.19
).

Proof.

(i) The claims (
2.12
)–(
2.14
) follow easily from definitions and (
2.2
).

(ii) To prove (
2.15
) we use the Poisson summation formula to write

\begin{split}\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{d}}\sum_{\xi\in({\mathbb{Z}}/R)^{d}}m(\xi)e^{ix\cdot\xi}&=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{d}}\sum_{\xi\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}m(\xi/R)e^{i(x/R)\cdot\xi}\\
&=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{d}}\sum_{a\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}m(\xi/R)e^{i(x/R)\cdot\xi}e^{-2\pi ia\cdot\xi}\,d\xi\\
&=\sum_{a\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\check{m}(x-2\pi Ra).\end{split}

The desired bound (
2.15
) follows.

(iii) In view of (
2.15
) we have

\big\|m(\xi)\varphi_{\underline{k}}(\xi)\big\|_{S^{\infty}}\lesssim\Big\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}m^{\prime}(\xi)\varphi_{\underline{k}}(\xi)e^{ix\cdot\xi}\,d\xi\Big\|_{L^{1}_{x}}.

(2.20)

We integrate by parts in
\xi
, and use the assumptions (
2.16
) and Plancherel theorem to see that

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}m^{\prime}(\xi)\varphi_{\underline{k}}(\xi)e^{ix\cdot\xi}\,d\xi\Big|\lesssim\frac{2^{k_{1}/2}\cdot\ldots\cdot 2^{k_{d}/2}}{(1+2^{k_{1}}|x_{1}|+\ldots+2^{k_{d}}|x_{d}|)^{(d+1)/2}}K(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})

(2.21)

for any
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where the kernel
K
satisfies
\|K\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\Lambda
. The desired bounds (
2.31
) follow using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

(iv) Without loss of generality we may assume that
k_{1}=\max(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})
. Recalling the definition (
2.9
) and using the bounds (
2.15
) it suffices to show that

\begin{split}\Big\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}&m^{\prime\prime}(-\xi_{2}-\ldots-\xi_{d+1},\xi_{2},\ldots,\xi_{d+1})\varphi_{k_{1}}(-\xi_{2}-\ldots-\xi_{d+1})\\
&\times\varphi_{k_{2}}(\xi_{2})\ldots\varphi_{k_{d+1}}(\xi_{d+1})e^{i(x_{2}\xi_{2}+\ldots+x_{d+1}\xi_{d+1})}\,d\xi_{2}\ldots d\xi_{d+1}\Big\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\Lambda.\end{split}

(2.22)

Using the assumptions (
2.18
), integration by parts, and the Plancherel theorem we have

\begin{split}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}m^{\prime\prime}(-\xi_{2}&-\ldots-\xi_{d+1},\xi_{2},\ldots,\xi_{d+1})\varphi_{k_{1}}(-\xi_{2}-\ldots-\xi_{d+1})\varphi_{k_{2}}(\xi_{2})\ldots\varphi_{k_{d+1}}(\xi_{d+1})\\
&\times e^{i(x_{2}\xi_{2}+\ldots+x_{d+1}\xi_{d+1})}\,d\xi_{2}\ldots d\xi_{d+1}\Big|\lesssim\frac{2^{k_{2}/2}\cdot\ldots\cdot 2^{k_{d+1}/2}\cdot K(x_{2},\ldots,x_{d+1})}{(1+2^{k_{2}}|x_{2}|+\ldots+2^{k_{d+1}}|x_{d+1}|)^{(d+1)/2}},\end{split}

where
\|K\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\Lambda
. The bounds (
2.22
) follow.
∎

The lemma above motivates the following definition:

Definition 2.3

(Hörmander-Mikhlin norm)
.

Given a multiplier
m:\mathbb{H}^{d}_{R}\to\mathbb{C}
,
a lattice point
\underline{k}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d+1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d+1}
, an integer
a\geq 1
,
and a number
\Lambda\in(0,\infty)
, we let

\varphi_{\underline{k}}(\xi):=\varphi_{k_{1}}(\xi_{1})\ldots\varphi_{k_{d+1}}(\xi_{d+1})
and write

\big\|m\cdot\varphi_{\underline{k}}\big\|_{\mathcal{HM}_{a}}\lesssim\Lambda

(2.23)

if
m
admits an extension
m^{\prime\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\to\mathbb{C}
such that

m(\xi)\varphi_{\underline{k}}(\xi)=m^{\prime\prime}(\xi)\varphi_{\underline{k}}(\xi)
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{R}^{d}
and

\big|2^{\alpha\cdot\underline{k}}\partial^{\alpha}_{\xi}\big[m^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d+1})\varphi_{k_{1}}(\xi_{1})\ldots\varphi_{k_{d+1}}(\xi_{d+1})\big]\big|\lesssim\Lambda

(2.24)

for any multi-index
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d+1})
with
|\alpha|\leq a

and any
\xi\in\mathbb{H}^{d}_{\infty}
.
Here
2^{\alpha\cdot\underline{k}}=2^{\alpha_{1}k_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{d}k_{d}}

and
\partial^{\alpha}_{\xi}=\partial^{\alpha_{1}}_{\xi_{1}}\ldots\partial^{\alpha_{d}}_{\xi_{d}}
.

2.3.
Function spaces

We control our solutions using two types of spaces: the
L^{2}
-based spaces
\dot{H}^{N,b}({\mathbb{T}}_{R})
and the
L^{\infty}
-based spaces
\dot{W}^{N,b}({\mathbb{T}}_{R})
, defined by the norms

\begin{split}\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{N,b}}&:=\Big\{\|P_{-\infty}f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}R^{-2b}+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\|P_{k}f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}(2^{2Nk}+2^{2bk})\Big\}^{1/2},\\
\|f\|_{\dot{W}^{N,b}}&:=\|P_{-\infty}f\|_{L^{\infty}}R^{-b}+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\|P_{k}f\|_{L^{\infty}}(2^{Nk}+2^{bk}),\end{split}

(2.25)

where
b\in[-1,1]
and
N\geq\max(b,0)
. Notice that
\dot{H}^{N,0}=H^{N}
(the standard Sobolev spaces on
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
). The exponent
b
is important as it allows us to measure accurately low frequency contributions, which is a key issue in the long-term dynamics of solutions. We notice also that the spaces
\dot{W}^{N,b}
are invariant under the action of the Hilbert transform.

For technical reasons, connected to the algebra properties in Lemma
2.4
(i) below, we use also an additional family of
L^{\infty}
-based Sobolev spaces
\widetilde{W}^{N}
, defined by the norms

\|f\|_{\widetilde{W}^{N}}:=\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{Nk}\|P_{k}f\|_{L^{\infty}}.

(2.26)

Recall the Sobolev embedding estimates

\|P_{k}f\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim(2^{k/2}+R^{-1/2})\|P_{k}f\|_{L^{2}}\qquad\text{ for any }k\in\mathbb{Z}\cup\{-\infty\}.

(2.27)

In particular, for any
N\geq 1
and
f\in H^{N+1}({\mathbb{T}}_{R})
,

\|f\|_{\widetilde{W}^{N}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{W}^{N,0}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\widetilde{W}^{N}}\ln\{2+\|f\|_{H^{N+1}}/\|f\|_{\widetilde{W}^{N}}\}.

(2.28)

For any
l\in\mathbb{R}
let
l^{+}:=\max(l,0)
and
l^{-}:=\min(l,0)
. We will often use the following two lemmas to estimate products of functions and multilinear integrals.

Lemma 2.4
.

(i) Assume
s_{0}\geq 4
,
s_{1}\in[1,s_{0}-1]
, and the functions
f,g:\mathbb{T}_{R}\to\mathbb{C}
satisfy the bounds

\|f\|_{H^{s_{0}}}+\|g\|_{H^{s_{0}}}\lesssim A_{2},\qquad\|f\|_{\widetilde{W}^{s_{1}}}+\|g\|_{\widetilde{W}^{s_{1}}}\lesssim A_{\infty},

(2.29)

for some numbers
A_{\infty}\lesssim A_{2}\in(0,\infty)
. If
M
is a bilinear operator defined as in (
2.7
) then

\|M(f,g)\|_{H^{s_{0}}}\lesssim A_{2}A_{\infty},\qquad\|M(f,g)\|_{\widetilde{W}^{s_{1}}}\lesssim A^{2}_{\infty},

(2.30)

provided that the multiplier
m:({\mathbb{Z}}/R)^{2}\to{\mathbb{C}}
of the operator
M
satisfies the bounds

\big\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}[m(\rho,\eta)\varphi^{\ast}_{k}(\rho)\varphi_{j}^{\ast}(\eta)\varphi_{l}^{\ast}(\rho+\eta)]\big\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{T}}_{R}^{2})}\lesssim 2^{s_{1}\min(k,j)}

(2.31)

for any
k,j,l\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}
, where
\varphi^{\ast}_{a}:=\varphi_{a}
if
a\geq 1
and
\varphi^{\ast}_{0}:=\varphi_{\leq 0}
. In particular

\|f\cdot g\|_{H^{s_{0}}}\lesssim A_{2}A_{\infty},\qquad\|f\cdot g\|_{\widetilde{W}^{s_{1}}}\lesssim A^{2}_{\infty}.

(2.32)

(ii) The bounds (
2.31
) hold if
m
extends to a
C^{2}
function
m^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
satisfying

2^{\alpha k}\big|\partial_{\rho}^{\alpha}[m^{\prime}(\rho,\eta)\varphi^{\ast}_{k}(\rho)\varphi_{j}^{\ast}(\eta)]\big|+2^{\alpha j}\big|\partial_{\eta}^{\alpha}[m^{\prime}(\rho,\eta)\varphi^{\ast}_{k}(\rho)\varphi_{j}^{\ast}(\eta)]\big|\lesssim 2^{s_{1}\min(k,j)},

(2.33)

for any
k,j\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}
,
\alpha\in\{0,1,2\}
, and
\rho,\eta\in\mathbb{R}
.

(iii) The bounds (
2.31
) also hold if we assume that

(1)
m(\rho,0)=m(0,\rho)=m(-\rho,\rho)=0
for all
\rho\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R
;

(2) for any
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{3}
there is a
C^{2}
function
m^{\prime\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{C}
such that

m(\rho,\eta)\varphi_{k_{1}}(\rho)\varphi_{k_{2}}(\eta)\varphi_{k_{3}}(\rho+\eta)=m^{\prime\prime}(\rho,\eta,-\rho-\eta)\varphi_{k_{1}}(\rho)\varphi_{k_{2}}(\eta)\varphi_{k_{3}}(\rho+\eta)

(2.34)

for all
\rho,\eta\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R
, and

\big|\varphi_{k_{1}}(\xi_{1})\varphi_{k_{2}}(\xi_{2})\varphi_{k_{3}}(\xi_{3})\cdot 2^{\alpha\cdot k}(\partial^{\alpha}_{\xi}m^{\prime\prime})(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\big|\lesssim 2^{s_{1}\min(k_{1}^{+},k_{2}^{+})}2^{\min(k_{1}^{-},k_{2}^{-},k_{3}^{-})/8}

(2.35)

for any multi-index
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
with
|\alpha|\leq 2
and any
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}=0
. Here
2^{\alpha\cdot k}=2^{\alpha_{1}k_{1}+\alpha_{2}k_{2}+\alpha_{3}k_{3}}
and
\partial^{\alpha}_{\xi}=\partial^{\alpha_{1}}_{\xi_{1}}\partial^{\alpha_{2}}_{\xi_{2}}\partial^{\alpha_{3}}_{\xi_{3}}
.

Proof.

(i) For
k\geq 0
and
p\in\{2,\infty\}
we estimate

\begin{split}\|P_{k}M(f,g)\|_{L^{p}}&\lesssim\|P_{k}M(P^{\prime}_{k}f,P_{\leq k-4}g)\|_{L^{p}}+\|P_{k}M(P_{\leq k-4}f,P^{\prime}_{k}g)\|_{L^{p}}\\
&+\sum_{a,b\geq k-4,\,|a-b|\leq 8}\|P_{k}M(P_{a}f,P_{b}g)\|_{L^{p}}\\
&\lesssim A_{\infty}\sum_{a\geq k-4}\big(\|P_{a}f\|_{L^{p}}+\|P_{a}g\|_{L^{p}}\big),\end{split}

using the assumption (
2.31
) and the bounds (
2.10
) (with the low frequency placed in
L^{\infty}
and the high frequency in
L^{p}
). Moreover, letting
P_{a}^{\ast}:=P_{a}
if
a\geq 1
and
P_{0}^{\ast}:=P_{\leq 0}
, we also have

\begin{split}\|P_{\leq 0}M(f,g)\|_{L^{p}}&\lesssim\sum_{a,b\geq 0,\,|a-b|\leq 8}\|P_{0}^{\ast}M(P^{\ast}_{a}f,P^{\ast}_{b}g)\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim A_{\infty}\sum_{a\geq 0}\big(\|P_{a}^{\ast}f\|_{L^{p}}+\|P_{a}^{\ast}g\|_{L^{p}}\big).\end{split}

The desired bounds (
2.30
) follow from these two estimates.

(ii) We show now that the differential assumptions (
2.33
) imply the
S^{\infty}
bounds (
2.31
). This is similar to the proof of Lemma
2.2
(iii). Indeed, in view of (
2.12
) and (
2.15
) we have

\begin{split}\big\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}[m(\rho,\eta)\varphi^{\ast}_{k}(\rho)\varphi_{j}^{\ast}(\eta)&\varphi_{l}^{\ast}(\rho+\eta)]\big\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{T}}_{R}^{2})}\lesssim\big\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}[m(\rho,\eta)\varphi^{\ast}_{k}(\rho)\varphi_{j}^{\ast}(\eta)]\big\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{T}}_{R}^{2})}\\
&\lesssim\Big\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}m^{\prime}(\rho,\eta)\varphi^{\ast}_{k}(\rho)\varphi_{j}^{\ast}(\eta)e^{i(x\rho+y\eta)}\,d\rho d\eta\Big\|_{L^{1}_{x,y}}.\end{split}

(2.36)

Let
\Lambda:=2^{s_{1}\min(k,j)}
. We integrate by parts in
\rho
and
\eta
, and use the assumptions (
2.33
) and the Plancherel theorem to see that, for any
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}m^{\prime}(\rho,\eta)\varphi^{\ast}_{k}(\rho)\varphi_{j}^{\ast}(\eta)e^{i(x\rho+y\eta)}\,d\rho d\eta\Big|\lesssim\frac{2^{k/2}2^{j/2}}{(1+2^{k}|x|)^{2}+(1+2^{j}|y|)^{2}}K(x,y)

(2.37)

where
K
satisfies
\|K\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\Lambda
. The bounds (
2.31
) follow using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

(iii) In view of Lemma
2.2
(iv), for any
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
we have

\big\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}[m(\rho,\eta)\varphi_{k_{1}}(\rho)\varphi_{k_{2}}(\eta)\varphi_{k_{3}}(\rho+\eta)]\big\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{T}}_{R}^{2})}\lesssim 2^{s_{1}\min(k_{1}^{+},k_{2}^{+})}2^{\min(k_{1}^{-},k_{2}^{-},k_{3}^{-})/8}.

(2.38)

The bounds (
2.31
) follow by summation if any of the parameters
k
,
j
, or
l
is equal to
0
.
∎

Lemma 2.5
.

Assume
d\geq 3
,
s_{0}\geq 4
,
s_{1}\in[1,s_{0}-1]
, and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}:\mathbb{T}_{R}\to\mathbb{C}
are functions that satisfy the bounds

\|f_{j}\|_{H^{s_{0}}}\lesssim A_{2},\qquad\|f_{j}\|_{\widetilde{W}^{s_{1}}}\lesssim A_{\infty}

(2.39)

for some numbers
A_{\infty}\lesssim A_{2}\in(0,\infty)
, and any
j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
. Then

\Big|\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{d-1}}\sum_{(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d})\in\mathbb{H}^{d-1}_{R}}\widehat{f_{1}}(\xi_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\widehat{f_{d}}(\xi_{d})m(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d})\Big|\lesssim A_{2}^{2}A_{\infty}^{d-2},

(2.40)

provided that the multiplier
m
vanishes when one of the arguments is equal to
0
, and

\begin{split}&\big\|m(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d})\varphi_{k_{1}}(\xi_{1})\ldots\varphi_{k_{d}}(\xi_{d})\big\|_{\widetilde{S}^{\infty}}\lesssim\Lambda(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d}),\\
&\Lambda(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d}):=2^{s_{0}\max(\widetilde{k}_{1},0)}2^{s_{0}\max(\widetilde{k}_{2},0)}2^{\min(\widetilde{k}_{d},0)/8}\prod_{j=3}^{d}2^{s_{1}\max(\widetilde{k}_{j},0)},\end{split}

(2.41)

for any
k_{1},\ldots,k_{d}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, where
\widetilde{k}_{1}\geq\widetilde{k}_{2}\geq\ldots\geq\widetilde{k}_{d}
is the decreasing rearrangement of the set
k_{1},\ldots,k_{d}
.

Proof.

For any
k_{1},\ldots,k_{d}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
let

I_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{d}}:=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{d-1}}\sum_{(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d})\in\mathbb{H}^{d-1}_{R}}\widehat{P_{k_{1}}f_{1}}(\xi_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\widehat{P_{k_{d}}f_{d}}(\xi_{d})m(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d}).

For
p\in\{2,\infty\}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
let

B_{k,p}:=\sum_{j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}}\|P^{\prime}_{k}f_{j}\|_{L^{p}}.

Using the assumption (
2.41
) and the bounds (
2.11
) we have

|I_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{d}}|\lesssim(2^{s_{0}\max(\widetilde{k}_{1},0)}B_{\widetilde{k}_{1},2})(2^{s_{0}\max(\widetilde{k}_{2},0)}B_{\widetilde{k}_{2},2})\prod_{j=3}^{d}(2^{s_{1}\max(\widetilde{k}_{j},0)}2^{\min(\widetilde{k}_{j},0)/(8d)}B_{\widetilde{k_{j}},\infty}).

Recall that
m
vanishes when one of the arguments is equal to
0
. The bounds (
2.40
) follow,

\Big|\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{d-1}}\sum_{(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d})\in\mathbb{H}^{d-1}_{R}}\widehat{f_{1}}(\xi_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\widehat{f_{d}}(\xi_{d})m(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d})\Big|\lesssim\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}}|I_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{d}}|\lesssim A_{2}^{2}A_{\infty}^{d-2},

as desired.
∎

2.4.
Elements of paradifferential calculus

Our main theorems are based on establishing suitable energy estimates. For this we use a paradifferential approach.

The paradifferential operators on
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
are defined for functions
f,g\in H^{1}({\mathbb{T}}_{R})
by the formula

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}\big(T_{f}g\big)(\xi)&:=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}\chi(\xi-\eta,\eta)\widehat{f}(\xi-\eta)\widehat{g}(\eta),\\
\chi(x,y)&:=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\varphi_{\leq k-20}(x)\varphi_{k}(y+x/2).\end{split}

(2.42)

Notice that

\chi(x,y)=\chi(x,z)\qquad\text{ if }x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}\text{ satisfy }x+y+z=0.

(2.43)

Using the formulas (
2.2
) we have

\int_{{\mathbb{T}}_{R}}T_{a}f\cdot g\,dx=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}_{R}}f\cdot T_{a}g\,dx=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{2}}\sum_{\xi,\eta\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}\chi(-\xi-\eta,\eta)\widehat{a}(-\xi-\eta)\widehat{f}(\eta)\widehat{g}(\xi),

(2.44)

for any functions
f,g,a\in H^{1}({\mathbb{T}}_{R})
. In addition
\overline{T_{f}g}=T_{\overline{f}}\overline{g}
, so the function
T_{f}g
is real-valued if both
f
and
g
are real-valued.

For any
f,g\in H^{1}({\mathbb{T}}_{R})
we define the bilinear operator

\mathcal{H}(f,g):=fg-T_{f}g-T_{g}f,

(2.45)

which can be rewritten in the Fourier space as

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}[\mathcal{H}(f,g)](\xi)&=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}\widetilde{\chi}(\xi-\eta,\eta)\widehat{f}(\xi-\eta)\widehat{g}(\eta),\\
\widetilde{\chi}(x,y)&:=1-\chi(x,y)-\chi(y,x).\end{split}

(2.46)

The remainder
\mathcal{H}
is smoothing in the sense that for any
a_{1},a_{2}>0

\begin{split}&{\|P_{\geq 0}\mathcal{H}(f,g)\|}_{H^{a_{1}+a_{2}}}\lesssim_{a_{1},a_{2}}\min\big\{\|f\|_{H^{a_{1}}}\|g\|_{\widetilde{W}^{a_{2}}},\,\|f\|_{\widetilde{W}^{a_{2}}}\|g\|_{H^{a_{1}}}\big\},\\
&{\|P_{\geq 0}\mathcal{H}(f,g)\|}_{\widetilde{W}^{a_{1}+a_{2}}}\lesssim_{a_{1},a_{2}}\|f\|_{\widetilde{W}^{a_{1}}}\|g\|_{\widetilde{W}^{a_{2}}}.\end{split}

(2.47)

3.
Paralinearization of the water waves system

In this section we consider smooth solutions of the system (
1.4
) on some time interval.
Our goal is to rewrite this system in a way that is amenable to energy estimates.

3.1.
The Dirichlet-Neumann operator and the good variable

Assume
h:{\mathbb{T}}_{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth function and let
\Omega:=\{(x,z)\in{\mathbb{T}}_{R}\times\mathbb{R}:\,z<h(x)\}
. Given a sufficiently smooth function
\phi:{\mathbb{T}}_{R}\to\mathbb{R}
let
\Phi
denote the (unique in a suitable space) harmonic function in
\Omega
, continuous on
\overline{\Omega}
, and satisfying
\Phi(x,h(x))=\phi(x)
. We define the Dirichlet-Neumann map as

G(h)\phi=\sqrt{1+h_{x}^{2}}\,(\nu\cdot\nabla\Phi)

(3.1)

where
\nu
denotes the outward pointing unit normal to the domain
\Omega
. The first result of this subsection is the following paralinearization of the Dirichlet-Neumann map.

Lemma 3.1
.

(i) Assume that
\varepsilon,A\in(0,\infty)
are numbers such that
\varepsilon\ll 1
and
\varepsilon\lesssim A
.
Assume that
h,\phi:{\mathbb{T}}_{R}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy the bounds

\|h\|_{H^{N_{0}}}+\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi\|_{H^{N_{0}-1/2}}\leq A,\qquad\|h\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{1},0}}+\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{1}-1/2,0}}\leq\varepsilon.

(3.2)

Then there is a unique harmonic map
\Phi\in C^{4}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
such that
\Phi(x,h(x))=\phi(x)
and
\nabla\Phi\in L^{2}(\Omega)
.

(ii) We define
G(h)\phi
as in (
3.1
) and let

\displaystyle B:=\frac{G(h)\phi+\partial_{x}h\partial_{x}\phi}{1+(\partial_{x}h)^{2}},\qquad V:=\partial_{x}\phi-B\partial_{x}h,\qquad\omega:=\phi-T_{B}h.

(3.3)

Then for any
F\in\big\{\partial_{x}\phi,\,G(h)\phi,\,B,\,V\big\}
we have

\|F\|_{H^{N_{0}-1}}\lesssim A,\qquad\|F\|_{\widetilde{W}^{N_{1}-1}}\lesssim\varepsilon.

(3.4)

(iii) We can paralinearize the Dirichlet-Neumann operator as

G(h)\phi=|\partial_{x}|\omega-\partial_{x}(T_{V}h)+G_{2}(\phi,h)+G_{3}(\phi,h,h)+G_{\geq 4},

(3.5)

where

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}[G_{2}(\phi,h)](\xi)&=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}q_{2}(\xi-\eta,\eta)\widehat{\phi}(\xi-\eta)\widehat{h}(\eta),\\
q_{2}(\rho,\eta)&:=\widetilde{\chi}(\rho,\eta)[(\eta+\rho)\rho-|\eta+\rho||\rho|],\end{split}

(3.6)

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}[G_{3}(\phi,h,h)](\xi)&=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{2}}\sum_{\rho,\eta\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}q_{3}(\xi-\eta-\rho,\eta,\rho)\widehat{\phi}(\xi-\eta-\rho)\widehat{h}(\eta)\widehat{h}(\rho),\\
q_{3}(\sigma,\eta,\rho)&:=\frac{|\sigma+\eta+\rho||\sigma|}{2}\Big\{[1-\chi(\sigma+\rho,\eta)][|\sigma+\rho|-|\sigma|-\rho\,\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma+\eta+\rho)]\\
&\qquad\qquad+[1-\chi(\sigma+\eta,\rho)][|\sigma+\eta|-|\sigma|-\eta\,\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma+\eta+\rho)]\\
&\qquad\qquad+\big[|\sigma|-\sigma\,\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma+\eta+\rho)\big]\Big\},\end{split}

(3.7)

\|G_{\geq 4}\|_{\dot{H}^{N_{0},-3/4}}\lesssim A\varepsilon^{3},\qquad\|G_{\geq 4}\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{1},-3/4}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{4}.

(3.8)

The operators
G_{2}
and
G_{3}
are smoothing in the sense that their symbols satisfy the identities

q_{2}(\rho,\eta)=0\qquad\text{ if }\,\,|\rho|\leq 2^{-40}|\eta|\,\,\text{ or }\,\,|\eta|\leq 2^{-40}|\rho|,

(3.9)

q_{3}(\sigma,\eta,\rho)=0\qquad\text{ if }\,\,|\sigma|+|\eta|\leq 2^{-40}|\rho|\,\,\text{ or }\,\,|\eta|+|\rho|\leq 2^{-40}|\sigma|\,\,\text{ or }\,\,|\rho|+|\sigma|\leq 2^{-40}|\eta|.

(3.10)

We will also need an extension of this lemma to the case of time evolutions. Assume that
T_{1}\leq T_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and
(h,\phi)\in C([T_{1},T_{2}]:H^{N_{0}}\times\dot{H}^{N_{0},1/2})
is a solution of the system (
1.4
). Moreover, assume that for any
t\in[T_{1},T_{2}]
we have

\begin{split}\|h(t)\|_{H^{N_{0}}}+\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi(t)\|_{H^{N_{0}-1/2}}+\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega(t)\|_{H^{N_{0}}}&\leq A,\\
\|h(t)\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{1},0}}+\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi(t)\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{1}-1/2,0}}+\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega(t)\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{1},0}}&\leq\varepsilon(t),\end{split}

(3.11)

where
A\in(0,\infty)
and
\varepsilon:[T_{1},T_{2}]\to[0,\infty)
is a continuous function satisfying
\varepsilon(t)\ll 1
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Assume that (
3.11
) hold and that
B,V
are as in (
3.3
).
Then we can expand

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}h=|\partial_{x}|\phi+\dot{h}_{2}+\dot{h}_{\geq 3},\qquad\dot{h}_{2}:=-\partial_{x}(h\partial_{x}\phi)-|\partial_{x}|(h|\partial_{x}|\phi),\\
&\partial_{t}\phi=-h+\dot{\phi}_{2}+\dot{\phi}_{\geq 3},\qquad\dot{\phi}_{2}:=-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{x}\phi)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(|\partial_{x}|\phi)^{2},\end{split}

(3.12)

and

\begin{split}&B=|\partial_{x}|\phi+B_{2}+B_{\geq 3},\qquad B_{2}:=-|\partial_{x}|(h|\partial_{x}|\phi)-h\partial_{x}^{2}\phi,\\
&V=\partial_{x}\phi+V_{2}+V_{\geq 3},\qquad V_{2}:=-\partial_{x}h|\partial_{x}|\phi,\end{split}

(3.13)

where the cubic remainders satisfy the bounds

\begin{split}\|\dot{h}_{\geq 3}(t)\|_{H^{N_{0}-1}}+\|\dot{\phi}_{\geq 3}(t)\|_{H^{N_{0}-1}}+\|B_{\geq 3}(t)\|_{H^{N_{0}-1}}+\|V_{\geq 3}(t)\|_{H^{N_{0}-1}}\lesssim A[\varepsilon(t)]^{2},\\
\|\dot{h}_{\geq 3}(t)\|_{\widetilde{W}^{N_{1}-1}}+\|\dot{\phi}_{\geq 3}(t)\|_{\widetilde{W}^{N_{1}-1}}+\|B_{\geq 3}(t)\|_{\widetilde{W}^{N_{1}-1}}+\|V_{\geq 3}(t)\|_{\widetilde{W}^{N_{1}-1}}\lesssim[\varepsilon(t)]^{3},\end{split}

(3.14)

for any
t\in[T_{1},T_{2}]
. Moreover,

\begin{split}\partial_{t}B&=-|\partial_{x}|h+\dot{B}_{2}+\dot{B}_{\geq 3},\\
\dot{B}_{2}&:=-\frac{1}{2}|\partial_{x}|(\partial_{x}\phi)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}|\partial_{x}|(|\partial_{x}|\phi)^{2}-|\partial_{x}|\phi\,\partial_{x}^{2}\phi+h\partial_{x}^{2}h+|\partial_{x}|(h|\partial_{x}|h),\end{split}

(3.15)

\partial_{t}V=-\partial_{x}h+\dot{V}_{2}+\dot{V}_{\geq 3},\qquad\dot{V}_{2}:=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}(\partial_{x}\phi)^{2}+\partial_{x}h|\partial_{x}|h,

(3.16)

and

\begin{split}\partial_{t}^{2}B&=-|\partial_{x}|^{2}\phi+\ddot{B}_{2}+\ddot{B}_{\geq 3},\\
\ddot{B}_{2}&:=2|\partial_{x}|(\partial_{x}h\,\partial_{x}\phi)+2|\partial_{x}|(|\partial_{x}|h\,|\partial_{x}|\phi)+\partial_{x}^{2}h\,|\partial_{x}|\phi+|\partial_{x}|h\,\partial_{x}^{2}\phi-2\partial_{x}h\,\partial_{x}|\partial_{x}|\phi,\end{split}

(3.17)

where the cubic remainders
\dot{B}_{\geq 3},\dot{V}_{\geq 3},\ddot{B}_{\geq 3}
satisfy the estimates

\begin{split}\|\dot{B}_{\geq 3}(t)\|_{H^{N_{0}-2}}+\|\dot{V}_{\geq 3}(t)\|_{H^{N_{0}-2}}+\|\ddot{B}_{\geq 3}(t)\|_{H^{N_{0}-3}}\lesssim A[\varepsilon(t)]^{2},\\
\|\dot{B}_{\geq 3}(t)\|_{\widetilde{W}^{N_{1}-2}}+\|\dot{V}_{\geq 3}(t)\|_{\widetilde{W}^{N_{1}-2}}+\|\ddot{B}_{\geq 3}(t)\|_{\widetilde{W}^{N_{1}-3}}\lesssim[\varepsilon(t)]^{3}.\end{split}

(3.18)

These lemmas are similar to Proposition B.1 in
[
37
]
.
For convenience, we provide details of the proofs in Section
8
.

3.2.
Evolution equations

We derive now an evolution equation for the good variable
\omega
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Assume that
T_{1}\leq T_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and
(h,\phi)\in C([T_{1},T_{2}]:H^{N_{0}}\times\dot{H}^{N_{0},1/2})
is a solution of the system (
1.4
) satisfying (
3.11
). Then the variable
\omega
satisfies the evolution equation

\partial_{t}\omega=-h-T_{\partial_{t}B+V\partial_{x}B}h-T_{V}\partial_{x}\omega+L_{2}(\phi,\phi)+L_{3}(h,\phi,\phi)+L_{\geq 4},

(3.19)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{F}[L_{2}(\phi,\phi)](\xi)&=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}\ell_{2}(\xi-\eta,\eta)\widehat{\phi}(\xi-\eta)\widehat{\phi}(\eta),\\
\ell_{2}(\rho,\eta)&:=\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\chi}(\rho,\eta)[|\eta||\rho|+\eta\rho],\end{split}

(3.20)

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}\big[L_{3}(h,\phi,\phi)\big](\xi)&=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{2}}\sum_{\rho,\eta\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}\ell_{3}(\xi-\eta-\rho,\eta,\rho)\widehat{h}(\xi-\eta-\rho)\widehat{\phi}(\eta)\widehat{\phi}(\rho),\\
\ell_{3}(\sigma,\eta,\rho)&:=\eta|\rho|\big\{(\sigma+\rho)\chi(\eta,\sigma+\rho)\chi(\rho,\sigma)+\sigma\chi(\sigma+\rho,\eta)-\sigma\chi(\rho,\sigma+\eta)\\
&\qquad\quad-\rho\chi(\eta+\rho,\sigma)+(|\rho|-|\sigma+\rho|)\mathrm{sgn}(\eta)\widetilde{\chi}(\sigma+\rho,\eta)\big\},\end{split}

(3.21)

and, for any
t\in[T_{1},T_{2}]
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}L_{\geq 4}(t)\|}_{\dot{H}^{N_{0},-1/4}}\lesssim A\varepsilon(t)^{3},\qquad{\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}L_{\geq 4}(t)\|}_{\dot{W}^{N_{1},-1/4}}\lesssim\varepsilon(t)^{4}.\end{split}

(3.22)

The operators
L_{2}
and
L_{3}
are smoothing in the sense that their symbols satisfy the identities

\ell_{2}(\rho,\eta)=0\qquad\text{ if }\,\,|\rho|\leq 2^{-40}|\eta|\,\,\text{ or }\,\,|\eta|\leq 2^{-40}|\rho|,

(3.23)

\ell_{3}(\sigma,\eta,\rho)=0\qquad\text{ if }\,\,|\sigma|+|\eta|\leq 2^{-40}|\rho|\,\,\text{ or }\,\,|\eta|+|\rho|\leq 2^{-40}|\sigma|\,\,\text{ or }\,\,|\rho|+|\sigma|\leq 2^{-40}|\eta|.

(3.24)

Proof.

We start from the second equation in (
1.4
) and use (
3.3
) to calculate

\begin{split}-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{x}\phi)^{2}+\frac{(G(h)\phi+\partial_{x}h\partial_{x}\phi)^{2}}{2(1+(\partial_{x}h)^{2})}&=\frac{1}{2}\big[B^{2}(1+(\partial_{x}h)^{2})-(V+B\partial_{x}h)^{2}\big]\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\big[B^{2}-2VB\partial_{x}h-V^{2}\big].\end{split}

(3.25)

Therefore, using the definition
\omega=\phi-T_{B}h
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}\omega=\partial_{t}\phi-T_{\partial_{t}B}h-T_{B}\partial_{t}h=-h-T_{\partial_{t}B}h+I+II,\end{split}

(3.26)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}I&:=(1/2)(B^{2}-V^{2})-VB\partial_{x}h,\qquad II:=-T_{B}(G(h)\phi).\end{split}

We use the identities
V+B\partial_{x}h=\partial_{x}\phi=\partial_{x}\omega+\partial_{x}T_{B}h
and recall (
2.45
) to calculate

\displaystyle\begin{split}I&=(B^{2}-V^{2})/2-VB\partial_{x}h\\
&=T_{B}B+(1/2)\mathcal{H}(B,B)-T_{V}V-(1/2)\mathcal{H}(V,V)-T_{V}(B\partial_{x}h)-T_{B\partial_{x}h}V-\mathcal{H}(V,B\partial_{x}h)\\
&=T_{B}B-T_{V}\partial_{x}\omega+(1/2)\mathcal{H}(|\partial_{x}|\phi,|\partial_{x}|\phi)-(1/2)\mathcal{H}(\partial_{x}\phi,\partial_{x}\phi)\\
&-T_{V}\big(\partial_{x}T_{B}h\big)-T_{B\partial_{x}h}V+\mathcal{H}(B,B-|\partial_{x}|\phi)+I_{\geq 4},\end{split}

(3.27)

where
\mathcal{H}
is defined as in (
2.45
) and

\displaystyle\begin{split}I_{\geq 4}&:=(1/2)\mathcal{H}(B,B)-(1/2)\mathcal{H}(|\partial_{x}|\phi,|\partial_{x}|\phi)-\mathcal{H}(B,B-|\partial_{x}|\phi)\\
&\,-(1/2)\mathcal{H}(V,V)+(1/2)\mathcal{H}(\partial_{x}\phi,\partial_{x}\phi)-\mathcal{H}(V,B\partial_{x}h).\end{split}

Using again
V=\partial_{x}\phi-B\partial_{x}h
we calculate

\displaystyle I_{\geq 4}=-(1/2)\mathcal{H}(B-|\partial_{x}|\phi,B-|\partial_{x}|\phi)+(1/2)\mathcal{H}(B\partial_{x}h,B\partial_{x}h),

which is an acceptable quartic remainder, due to (
2.47
).

From (
3.3
) we have
B-V\partial_{x}h=G(h)\phi
so that
II=-T_{B}B+T_{B}(V\partial_{x}h)
.
Then,

\displaystyle\begin{split}I+II&=-T_{V}\partial_{x}\omega+(1/2)\mathcal{H}(|\partial_{x}|\phi,|\partial_{x}|\phi)-(1/2)\mathcal{H}(\partial_{x}\phi,\partial_{x}\phi)\\
&+\big[-T_{V}(\partial_{x}T_{B}h)+T_{B}(V\partial_{x}h)-T_{B\partial_{x}h}V\big]+\mathcal{H}(B,B-|\partial_{x}|\phi)+I_{\geq 4}.\end{split}

(3.28)

The term
-T_{V}\partial_{x}\omega
is the same as in (
3.19
), while the
\mathcal{H}
terms in the first line of (
3.28
) give
L_{2}(\phi,\phi)
as in (
3.20
). For the terms in the second line we can write

\displaystyle-T_{V}(\partial_{x}T_{B}h)+T_{B}(V\partial_{x}h)-T_{B\partial_{x}h}V=-T_{V\partial_{x}B}h+R_{1}(V,B,h)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{F}\big[R_{1}(V,B,h)\big](\xi)&=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{2}}\sum_{\rho,\eta\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}\widehat{V}(\eta)\widehat{B}(\rho)\widehat{h}(\xi-\eta-\rho)r_{1}(\xi,\eta,\rho),\\
r_{1}(\xi,\eta,\rho)&:=-\chi(\eta,\xi-\eta)i(\xi-\eta)\chi(\rho,\xi-\eta-\rho)+\chi(\rho,\xi-\rho)i(\xi-\eta-\rho)\\
&\quad\,\,\,-\chi(\xi-\eta,\eta)i(\xi-\eta-\rho)+\chi(\eta+\rho,\xi-\eta-\rho)i\rho.\end{split}

(3.29)

Notice that
R_{1}(h,V,B)
is a cubic term which is smoothing,
in the sense that
r_{1}(\xi,\eta,\rho)=0
if
|\eta|+|\rho|\leq 2^{-40}|\xi|

or if
|\eta|+|\xi-\eta-\rho|\leq 2^{-40}|\xi|
or if
|\rho|+|\xi-\eta-\rho|\leq 2^{-40}|\xi|
.
Therefore we can replace
V
with
\partial_{x}\phi
and
B
with
|\partial_{x}|\phi

at the expense of acceptable quartic errors. Moreover, in view of (
3.13
)
we may replace the remaining term
\mathcal{H}(B,B-|\partial_{x}|\phi)
in (
3.28
)
with
\mathcal{H}(|\partial_{x}|\phi,-|\partial_{x}|(h|\partial_{x}|\phi)-h\partial_{x}^{2}\phi)

at the expense of acceptable quartic remainders.
A simple calculation shows that the resulting smoothing cubic terms are
of the form
L_{3}(h,\phi,\phi)
as claimed in (
3.21
).
∎

We define now our main variables
h^{\ast}
and
\omega^{\ast}
. These variables are optimal for our analysis, in particular for combining normal forms and energy estimates (without loss of derivatives) for two main reasons: (1) they satisfy the good paradifferential evolution equations (
3.30
), with well-defined quasi-linear and semi-linear terms, and (2) they are both at the level of derivatives of the Hamiltonian .

Lemma 3.4
.

Assume that
T_{1}\leq T_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and
(h,\phi)\in C([T_{1},T_{2}]:H^{N_{0}}\times\dot{H}^{N_{0},1/2})

is a solution of the system (
1.4
) satisfying (
3.11
).
We define

\begin{split}&\alpha:=\partial_{t}B+V\partial_{x}B,\qquad\beta:=\sqrt{1+\alpha}-1,\\
&h^{\ast}:=T_{\sqrt{1+\alpha}}h=h+T_{\beta}h,\qquad\omega^{\ast}:=|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega.\end{split}

(3.30)

Then the variables
h^{\ast},\omega^{\ast}
satisfy the evolution equations

\begin{split}\partial_{t}h^{\ast}-|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega^{\ast}&=T_{\beta}|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega^{\ast}-\partial_{x}T_{V}h^{\ast}+H_{2}^{\ast}+H^{\ast}_{\geq 3}=:\mathcal{N}_{\geq 2}^{h^{\ast}},\\
\partial_{t}\omega^{\ast}+|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}h^{\ast}&=-T_{\beta}|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}h^{\ast}-\partial_{x}T_{V}\omega^{\ast}+\Omega_{2}^{\ast}+\Omega^{\ast}_{\geq 3}=:\mathcal{N}_{\geq 2}^{\omega^{\ast}},\end{split}

(3.31)

where

\begin{split}&H_{2}^{\ast}=H^{ri}_{2}(h^{\ast},\omega^{\ast}),\qquad\mathcal{F}\{H^{ri}_{2}(h^{\ast},\omega^{\ast})\}(\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}\widehat{h^{\ast}}(\xi-\eta)\widehat{\omega^{\ast}}(\eta)n^{ri}(\xi-\eta,\eta),\\
&n^{ri}(\rho,\eta):=\widetilde{\chi}(\eta,\rho)\frac{(\rho+\eta)\eta-|\rho+\eta||\eta|}{|\eta|^{1/2}}-\chi(\eta,\rho)\frac{|\eta|^{3/2}}{2},\end{split}

(3.32)

\Omega_{2}^{\ast}=\Omega_{2}^{rr}(h^{\ast},h^{\ast})+\Omega^{ii}_{2}(\omega^{\ast},\omega^{\ast}),

(3.33)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{F}\{\Omega_{2}^{rr}(h^{\ast},h^{\ast})\}(\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}\widehat{h^{\ast}}(\xi-\eta)\widehat{h^{\ast}}(\eta)n^{rr}(\xi-\eta,\eta),\\
&n^{rr}(\rho,\eta):=\chi(\rho,\eta)\frac{|\rho|}{2}(|\rho+\eta|^{1/2}-|\eta|^{1/2}),\end{split}

(3.34)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{F}\{\Omega^{ii}_{2}(\omega^{\ast},\omega^{\ast})\}(\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}\widehat{\omega^{\ast}}(\xi-\eta)\widehat{\omega^{\ast}}(\eta)n^{ii}(\xi-\eta,\eta),\\
&n^{ii}(\rho,\eta):=\widetilde{\chi}(\rho,\eta)|\rho+\eta|^{1/2}\frac{|\eta||\rho|+\eta\rho}{2|\eta|^{1/2}|\rho|^{1/2}}+\chi(\rho,\eta)\rho\frac{|\rho+\eta|^{1/2}\eta-|\eta|^{1/2}(\rho+\eta)}{|\eta|^{1/2}|\rho|^{1/2}},\end{split}

(3.35)

and the cubic remainders
H^{\ast}_{\geq 3},\Omega^{\ast}_{\geq 3}
satisfy the bounds

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\|H^{\ast}_{\geq 3}(t)\|}_{H^{N_{0}}}+{\|\Omega^{\ast}_{\geq 3}(t)\|}_{H^{N_{0}}}&\lesssim A\varepsilon(t)^{2},\\
{\|H^{\ast}_{\geq 3}(t)\|}_{\widetilde{W}^{N_{1}}}+{\|\Omega^{\ast}_{\geq 3}(t)\|}_{\widetilde{W}^{N_{1}}}&\lesssim\varepsilon(t)^{3},\end{split}

(3.36)

for any
t\in[T_{1},T_{2}]
.

Proof.

We use the equations (
3.19
) and
\partial_{t}h=G(h)\phi
, and the identities (
3.5
). Thus

\begin{split}\partial_{t}h^{\ast}&=T_{\partial_{t}\sqrt{1+\alpha}}h+T_{\sqrt{1+\alpha}}|\partial_{x}|\omega-T_{\sqrt{1+\alpha}}\partial_{x}T_{V}h+T_{\sqrt{1+\alpha}}G_{2}(\phi,h)+H_{\geq 3}^{1},\\
\partial_{t}\omega^{\ast}&=-|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}T_{1+\alpha}h-|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}T_{V}\omega_{x}+|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}L_{2}(\phi,\phi)+\Omega_{\geq 3}^{1},\end{split}

where
H_{\geq 3}^{1},\Omega_{\geq 3}^{1}
are acceptable cubic remainders satisfying (
3.36
). Therefore

\begin{split}\partial_{t}h^{\ast}-T_{\sqrt{1+\alpha}}|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega^{\ast}+\partial_{x}T_{V}h^{\ast}&=\big\{\partial_{x}T_{V}T_{\sqrt{1+\alpha}}h-T_{\sqrt{1+\alpha}}\partial_{x}T_{V}h\}\\
&+T_{\sqrt{1+\alpha}}G_{2}(\phi,h)+T_{\partial_{t}\sqrt{1+\alpha}}h+H_{\geq 3}^{1},\end{split}

(3.37)

and

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\omega^{\ast}+T_{\sqrt{1+\alpha}}|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}h^{\ast}&+\partial_{x}T_{V}\omega^{\ast}=\big\{T_{\sqrt{1+\alpha}}|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}T_{\sqrt{1+\alpha}}h-|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}T_{1+\alpha}h\big\}\\
&+\{\partial_{x}T_{V}|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega-|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}T_{V}\omega_{x}\}+|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}L_{2}(\phi,\phi)+\Omega_{\geq 3}^{1}.\end{split}

(3.38)

Using the formula
\alpha:=\partial_{t}B+V\partial_{x}B
and Lemma
3.2
we can expand

\begin{split}&\alpha=-|\partial_{x}|h+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{\geq 3},\\
&\alpha_{2}:=-\frac{1}{2}|\partial_{x}|(\partial_{x}\phi)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}|\partial_{x}|(|\partial_{x}|\phi)^{2}-|\partial_{x}|\phi\partial_{x}^{2}\phi+\partial_{x}\phi\partial_{x}|\partial_{x}|\phi+h\partial_{x}^{2}h+|\partial_{x}|(h|\partial_{x}|h),\\
&\|\alpha_{\geq 3}(t)\|_{\widetilde{W}^{N_{1}-2}}\lesssim\varepsilon(t)^{3}\qquad\text{ for any }t\in[T_{1},T_{2}],\end{split}

(3.39)

and

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\alpha=-|\partial_{x}|^{2}\phi+\dot{\alpha}_{2}+\dot{\alpha}_{\geq 3},\\
&\dot{\alpha}_{2}:=2|\partial_{x}|[\partial_{x}h\,\partial_{x}\phi+|\partial_{x}|h\,|\partial_{x}|\phi]+\partial_{x}^{2}h\,|\partial_{x}|\phi+|\partial_{x}|h\,\partial_{x}^{2}\phi-3\partial_{x}h\,\partial_{x}|\partial_{x}|\phi-\partial_{x}|\partial_{x}|h\,\partial_{x}\phi,\\
&\|\dot{\alpha}_{\geq 3}(t)\|_{\widetilde{W}^{N_{1}-3}}\lesssim\varepsilon(t)^{3}\qquad\text{ for any }t\in[T_{1},T_{2}].\end{split}

(3.40)

Since
\beta=\sqrt{1+\alpha}-1
we have
(1+\beta)^{2}=1+\alpha
, therefore

\begin{split}T_{\sqrt{1+\alpha}}|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}T_{\sqrt{1+\alpha}}h-|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}T_{1+\alpha}h&=\big\{T_{\beta}|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}h-|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}T_{\beta}h\big\}\\
&+\big\{T_{\beta}|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}T_{\beta}h-|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}T_{\beta^{2}}h\big\}.\end{split}

Using the definitions and (
3.39
) we therefore have

\begin{split}&T_{\sqrt{1+\alpha}}|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}T_{\sqrt{1+\alpha}}h-|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}T_{1+\alpha}h=\Omega_{2}^{1}(h^{\ast},h^{\ast})+\Omega_{\geq 3}^{2},\\
&\mathcal{F}\{\Omega_{2}^{1}(h^{\ast},h^{\ast})\}(\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}\widehat{h^{\ast}}(\xi-\eta)\widehat{h^{\ast}}(\eta)\chi(\xi-\eta,\eta)\frac{|\xi-\eta|}{2}(|\xi|^{1/2}-|\eta|^{1/2}),\end{split}

where
\Omega_{\geq 3}^{2}
is an acceptable cubic remainder. Similarly, using also the approximate identities in Lemma
3.2
, we can rewrite the other terms in the right-hand side of (
3.38
) as

\begin{split}&\partial_{x}T_{V}|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega-|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}T_{V}\partial_{x}\omega=\Omega_{2}^{2,1}(\omega^{\ast},\omega^{\ast})+\Omega_{\geq 3}^{3},\\
&\mathcal{F}\{\Omega_{2}^{2,1}(\omega^{\ast},\omega^{\ast})\}(\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}\widehat{\omega^{\ast}}(\xi-\eta)\widehat{\omega^{\ast}}(\eta)\chi(\xi-\eta,\eta)(\xi-\eta)\frac{|\xi|^{1/2}\eta-|\eta|^{1/2}\xi}{|\eta|^{1/2}|\xi-\eta|^{1/2}},\end{split}

\begin{split}&|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}L_{2}(\phi,\phi)=\Omega_{2}^{2,2}(\omega^{\ast},\omega^{\ast})+\Omega_{\geq 3}^{4},\\
&\mathcal{F}\{\Omega_{2}^{2,2}(\omega^{\ast},\omega^{\ast})\}(\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}\widehat{\omega^{\ast}}(\xi-\eta)\widehat{\omega^{\ast}}(\eta)\widetilde{\chi}(\xi-\eta,\eta)|\xi|^{1/2}\frac{|\eta||\xi-\eta|+\eta(\xi-\eta)}{2|\eta|^{1/2}|\xi-\eta|^{1/2}},\end{split}

for some acceptable cubic remainders
\Omega_{\geq 3}^{3},\Omega_{\geq 3}^{4}
. Similarly, the terms in the right-hand side of (
3.37
) can be rewritten as

\partial_{x}T_{V}T_{\sqrt{1+\alpha}}h-T_{\sqrt{1+\alpha}}\partial_{x}T_{V}h=H_{\geq 3}^{2},

\begin{split}&T_{\sqrt{1+\alpha}}G_{2}(\phi,h)=H_{2}^{1}(h^{\ast},\omega^{\ast})+H_{\geq 3}^{3},\\
&\mathcal{F}\{H_{2}^{1}(h^{\ast},\omega^{\ast})\}(\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}\widehat{h^{\ast}}(\xi-\eta)\widehat{\omega^{\ast}}(\eta)\widetilde{\chi}(\eta,\xi-\eta)\frac{\xi\eta-|\xi||\eta|}{|\eta|^{1/2}},\end{split}

\begin{split}&T_{\partial_{t}\sqrt{1+\alpha}}h=H_{2}^{2}(h^{\ast},\omega^{\ast})+H_{\geq 3}^{4},\\
&\mathcal{F}\{H_{2}^{2}(h^{\ast},\omega^{\ast})\}(\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R}\widehat{h^{\ast}}(\xi-\eta)\widehat{\omega^{\ast}}(\eta)\chi(\eta,\xi-\eta)(-|\eta|^{3/2}/2),\end{split}

for some acceptable cubic remainders
H_{\geq 3}^{2},H_{\geq 3}^{3},H_{\geq 3}^{4}
.
The desired quadratic formulas (
3.32
)–(
3.35
) follow by collecting the terms in the formulas above.
∎

4.
Energy estimates and normal forms

We are now ready to start the proof of Theorem
1.1
. Assume that
T_{1}\leq T_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and
(h,\phi)\in C([T_{1},T_{2}]:H^{N_{0}}\times\dot{H}^{N_{0},1/2})
